{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.57 L 5.66 9.23 L 11.32 3.57 L 5.66 -2.09 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}T)=1)=1))
(see, e.g.,
[
8
, Th. 10.47]
).

Both
HyperProb
and
HyperPAYNT
search for policies and restrict themselves to MD schedulers. Solving a property over MD schedulers is not always equivalent to checking it over general schedulers (see, e.g.,
\Cref
th:general_necessary,
\Cref
ex:oneschedtwostate-exists-greater-memory), but it coincides sometimes (
\Cref
th:MD_suffice).
Further,
HyperProb
supports only properties with a single scheduler quantifier (i.e., all probability operators are evaluated over the same MD scheduler).

HyperPAYNT
, on the other hand, supports an arbitrary number of scheduler quantifiers.
12
12

12

For both
HyperProb
and
HyperPAYNT
, one could alternatively consider a single scheduler on a manually created self-composition to simulate multiple schedulers (from the same initial state). Based on the large performance gap with
HyperProb
and
HyperPAYNT
seen below, we did not consider such tweaks.

To account for the different scheduler classes considered by the tools, we will in the following denote the validity of a property over general as well as over MD schedulers as
HR result
and
MD result
, respectively, when comparing the tools.
13
13

13

Note that
HyperPAYNT
expects existentially quantified properties, but we report the validity w.r.t. the universally quantified property, so the MD result stated here is the opposite of the result reported by
HyperPAYNT
.

We will also denote for each benchmark family whether the considered property belongs to a fragment for which checking MD schedulers is equivalent to checking general schedulers.

Our tool’s core procedure
for treating relational reachability properties

consists of polynomially many calls to well-established, practically efficient subroutines based on value iteration
[
47
]
.
In contrast,
HyperProb
and
HyperPAYNT
use exponential algorithms to solve the
NP
-hard
RelReach
problems over MD schedulers and do not optimize for the
PTIME
special cases (
\Cref
th:general_PTIME).
Further, to optimize a linear combination of expected rewards over different schedulers, we can treat the different state-scheduler combinations independently and then aggregate them in Step 4 of
\Cref
sec:reach_algo (p.
5.1
).

8.2
Case Studies

Let us first illustrate that relational properties cover interesting problems.

Generalization of Von Neumann’s trick (
VN
).

We generalize Von Neumann’s trick from
\Cref
ex:reach_motivating-ex to
2N
bits.
The idea is as follows:
Extract the first
2N
bits from the stream; if the number of zeros equals the number of ones, return the value of the first bit; otherwise try again.
We check whether

\forall\sigma.\ {\text{Pr}}_{s_{0}}^{\sigma}(\operatorname{\hbox to8.7pt{\vbox to8.7pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.76527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.57 L 5.66 9.23 L 11.32 3.57 L 5.66 -2.09 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\{\texttt{return 0}\})\approx_{\epsilon}{\text{Pr}}_{s_{0}}^{\sigma}(\operatorname{\hbox to8.7pt{\vbox to8.7pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.76527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.57 L 5.66 9.23 L 11.32 3.57 L 5.66 -2.09 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\{\texttt{return 1}\})

for
\epsilon=0
and
\epsilon=0.1
and varying values for
N
.
By
\Cref
th:MD_suffice
(b)
, checking this property over MD schedulers is equivalent to checking it over general schedulers since all target sets are absorbing.
Therefore,
HyperProb
and
HyperPAYNT
can also check the general problem in this case even though they restrict to MD schedulers.

Robot tag (
RT
).

Consider a
N{\times}N
grid world with a robot and a janitor, where both move in turns, starting with the robot.
14
14

14

Our model is based on a
PRISM
model
and a
Gridworld-By-Storm model
.

The robot starts in the lower left corner, the janitor in the upper right corner.
The robot has fixed the following strategy: It moves right until it reaches the lower right corner, then moves up until it reaches the upper right corner, its target.
The janitor can hinder the robot from reaching its target by occupying a cell that the robot wants to move to.
We now want to check whether this strategy is approximately robust against adversarial behavior by the janitor,
in the sense that the probability of reaching the target should be approximately independent of the janitor strategy.
Formally,

\forall\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}.\ {\text{Pr}}^{\sigma_{1}}_{s}(\operatorname{\hbox to8.7pt{\vbox to8.7pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.76527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.57 L 5.66 9.23 L 11.32 3.57 L 5.66 -2.09 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\{t\})~\approx_{10^{-5}}~{\text{Pr}}^{\sigma_{2}}_{s}(\operatorname{\hbox to8.7pt{\vbox to8.7pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.76527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.57 L 5.66 9.23 L 11.32 3.57 L 5.66 -2.09 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\{t\})~,

where
s
represents the initial state with robot and janitor in their initial locations, and

t
represents that the robot has reached its target.
We check this property for different grid sizes
N
and different starting positions for the janitor:
If the janitor starts in the upper right corner, they cannot hinder the robot, but if they start in location
(N,N{-}1)
, then they can always stop the robot.

By
\Cref
th:MD_suffice
(c)
, MD schedulers suffice here.
However,
HyperProb
does not natively support this problem as it only allows a single scheduler quantifier and the property is trivially satisfied if we use a single scheduler quantifier for both probability operators.

Israeli-Jalfon (Ĳ).

Recall Israeli & Jalfon’s self-stabilizing protocol and its asymmetric variant introduced in
\Cref
ex:buechi_motivating-ex,
15
15

15

We extend a
PRISM model
.
and that we are interested in the following conjunction of relational Büchi properties:

\displaystyle\bigwedge_{s_{0}\in S}\bigwedge_{i=1}^{N-1}\forall\sigma\in\Sigma^{\mathcal{M}}.\ {\text{Pr}}^{\sigma}_{s_{0}}(\operatorname{\hbox to6.98pt{\vbox to6.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.11945pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -1.19 L 0 7.74 L 8.94 7.74 L 8.94 -1.19 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }

Here, we benchmark the conjunct where
s_{0}
is the state where all processes have a token
16
16

16

and

for all tools for a varying number of processes

.
Observe that memoryless deterministic schedulers suffice here due to
\Cref

Results.

The results for both case studies for relational reachability properties,
VN
and
RT
, are presented in
\Cref
tab:eval_new.

Firstly, we observe that the quality of the coin simulation decreases with a growing number of bits
N
: While approximate equality with
\epsilon{=}0.1
holds for
N{=}10
, it does not hold anymore for
N{=}100
.

Further, our tool handles 1 million states in
6s
for
RT
but times out for
VN
instances of a quarter the size.

Besides the size of the state space, the computation time of expected rewards also depends on structural aspects, e.g. many cycles (as in
VN
) cause slower convergence of the approximation algorithm for expected rewards.

We note that our prototype performs worse on
VN
than our previous prototype from
[
28
]
, even though the implementation for solving relational reachability properties is the same in both versions.
We therefore attribute the decreased performance to changes in
\storm
: For
[
28
]
we used
\storm
1.9.0, for the current evaluation we use
\storm
1.12.0, the most recent version.

Still, on problem instances that are also supported by
HyperProb
or
HyperPAYNT
, our tool performs drastically better:
HyperProb
times out already for
VN
with
N{=}10
, and
HyperPAYNT
for
VN
with
N{=}100
while our tool solves these instances in a matter of seconds.

\Cref

tab:eval_buechi shows the results for the case study for relational Büchi properties, Ĳ.
Recall that
HyperPAYNT
does not support Büchi objectives.
Our tool handles roughly
2\,000\,000
states in around
35
minutes, only less than
1
minute of which consist of the actual computation time excluding model building.
It times out while building the model for over
4\,000\,000
states, while
HyperProb
times out for roughly
500
states already.

Benchmarks for Probabilistic Hyperproperties

Next, we investigate the scalability of our tool on benchmarks from the literature on probabilistic hyperproperties that are relational reachability properties. These benchmarks are typically motivated by security use cases.
In particular, three out of four
HyperPCTL
case studies for MDPs presented in
[
2
]
are covered by our approach:

from
[

Results.

\Cref

tab:eval presents our
experimental
results on these four case studies and provides a comparison with
HyperProb
and
HyperPAYNT
.
We observe that for all benchmarks in
\Cref
tab:eval, the running time of our tool consists almost entirely of building the model.

For the benchmarks with a single scheduler quantifier (
TA(1)
,
PW(1)
,
TS
,
SD
), checking over general schedulers is, in general, not equivalent to checking over MD schedulers (see
\Cref
ex:oneschedtwostate-exists-greater-memory).
Notably, already instances with
100\,000
states prove to be challenging over MD schedulers for
HyperProb
and
HyperPAYNT
, while our tool solves these instances over general schedulers in over a minute and can handle instances of
TA(1)
with 1 million states in less than 10 minutes.

For the benchmarks with two scheduler quantifiers (
TA(2)
and
PW(2)
),
checking over MD schedulers is equivalent to checking over general schedulers since both probability operators are independent (
\Cref
th:MD_suffice).
18
18

18

Checking for two scheduler quantifiers on a manually created self-composition of the MDP with
HyperProb
already exceeds memory bounds for
TA
with
m{=}8
.

Notably, our tool solves the instance for
M{=}24
in over a minute while
HyperPAYNT
times out.
Our tool can solve instances of
TA(2)
with more than 1 million states, but times out for the instance of
PW(2)
with almost 1 million states.

In summary, our tool is orders of magnitude faster than existing tools, which restrict to MD schedulers but nevertheless solve an equivalent problem in some cases (
TA(2)
,
PW(2)
).
For problem instances belonging to fragments whose decision problems are
NP
-hard over MD schedulers but in
PTIME
over general schedulers, we show that solving the
NP
-hard problem via SMT solving (
HyperProb
) or an abstraction-refinement approach (
HyperPAYNT
) is also much harder in practice than solving the
PTIME
problem.

9
Related Work

We discuss two main areas of related work:
multi-objective MDP model checking
and
probabilistic hyperlogics
.

Multi-objective model checking.

The techniques and results of this paper are closely related to
multi-objective model checking
(MOMC), a well-established area of research in probabilistic verification for MDPs
[
12
,
13
,
22
,
51
]
.

A key problem within MOMC is
multi-objective achievability
(MOA; see also
\Cref
sec:moa).
For reachability, the basic MOA question is:

Is there a scheduler such that target set
A
is reached with probability
at least

\lambda_{A}

and
target set
B
is reached with probability
at least

\lambda_{B}
?

Our relational properties, as defined in
\Cref
sec:problem-statement, subsume MOA queries with reachability, safety, Büchi, and coBüchi objectives.
This subsumption is strict:
For instance, no MOA query—nor any other MOMC formalism that we are aware of—can express the prototypical
RelReach
property that a scheduler reaches
A
and
B
with
equal
probabilities.

Similar to our results for
RelReach
, MOA with a fixed number of reachability objectives is
NP
-complete over MD schedulers
[
51
]
and in
PTIME
over general schedulers
[
22
]
.
Moreover, prominent algorithms for MOA
[
25
]
also rely on optimizing weighted sums of probabilities, just like our algorithm for
RelReach
.
The probabilistic model checking tools
PRISM

[
40
]
and
\storm

[
32
]
support various types of MOA queries.
Due to the algorithmic similarities, we were able to reuse some of
\storm
’s MOA capabilities in our implementation for
RelReach
.

The relation of our work to MOA goes further:
Our solution for
MORelReach
(see
\Cref
sec:conjunction) reduces the problem to MOA for total expected reward objectives.
The reduction constructs queries with both
positive and negative
rewards, and with
strict and non-strict
comparison operators, including
\not\approx_{\epsilon}
and
\neq
.
However, to the best of our knowledge, this particular type of MOA query is not covered by existing work:

•

Etessami et al.
[
22
]
address only
\omega
-regular objectives, including reachability as a special case, and comparison operators
<
,
\leq
,
=
,
\geq
, and
>
.

•

Forejt et al.
[
24
]
extend
[
22
]
to total expected reward with non-negative action-based payoffs.

•

Forejt, Kwiatkowska, and Parker
[
25
]
study MOA for reachability or total expected reward, again with non-negative reward functions only, and non-strict comparison operators;
their method explores the Pareto curve using value iteration.

•

The previous approach
[
25
]
was extended later to either entirely positive or entirely negative reward functions, as well as strict and non-strict comparison operators
[
50
,
48
]
.

•

Quatmann
[
51
]
treats MOA queries for general objectives expressed as measurable functions—this includes total expected reward objectives with mixed rewards—but restricts to the comparison operator
\geq
.

Since the specific MOA problem arising from our reduction is not covered by the literature, we present a dedicated solution in
\Cref
sec:moa.
Our approach relies on the techniques presented in
[
22
,
24
]
and is tailored to the specific structure of the MDPs constructed during the reduction.

Beyond MOA, our relational properties overlap with the
multi-dimensional percentile queries
studied in
[
52
]
(also see
\Cref
remark:buechi_sim-a-s), but neither subsumes the other:
We allow weighted sums with real-valued coefficients, whereas percentile queries cover several
payoff functions
such as
\inf
,
\sup
, and discounted sum.

Finally, somewhat tangential to MOMC,
distributional properties
for MDPs have also been studied
[
5
]
.
These properties can express, for example, that a scheduler induces a DTMC which, after a
finite
number of steps, reaches a distribution in which two given target sets have equal probability
[
4
]
.
In contrast,
RelReach
only considers reachability in the limit.
A more thorough comparison is beyond the scope of this paper, but it appears to be a promising direction for future work.

Probabilistic hyperlogics.

Our paper was motivated by recent emerging interest in algorithms for probabilistic
hyperlogics, in particular
HyperPCTL

[
3
,
2
]
and

PHL

[
18
]
.

The class of relational formulas defined in
\Cref
sec:problem-statement is

a strict fragment of
HyperPCTL
.
19
19

19

Recall that repeated reachability probabilities are PCTL-definable (see, e.g.,
[
8
, Th. 10.47]
).

While
PHL
cannot naturally compare probabilities from different initial states, every MDP
\mathcal{M}
and relational property
\varphi
can be transformed to an MDP
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
and
PHL
formula
\varphi^{\prime}
s.t.
\mathcal{M}
satisfies
\varphi
iff
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
satisfies
\varphi^{\prime}
over general schedulers (by making a copy of the MDP for every state-scheduler combination).

HyperPCTL
and
PHL
both can express properties like
Does there exist a scheduler such that all paths are trace-equivalent almost-surely?
, which relational formulas does not cover.
Due to their high expressiveness, the corresponding model-checking problems are undecidable.
This paper contributes two main points to the study of probabilistic hyperlogics.
First, searching for randomized and memoryful schedulers may be beneficial complexity-wise, both in theory and in practice.
Second, many of the motivating case studies for probabilistic hyperlogics can also be treated by dedicated and therefore much more efficient routines, which also motivated the use of an AR-loop in
[
6
]
.
However, in
[
6
]
a
search
over a finite amount of (MD) schedulers is suggested, while we study the computational complexity and consider an algorithm for general (and thus uncountably many) schedulers.
While
UPPAAL-SMC
is not a tool for probabilistic hyperproperties, it also supports
statistical
model checking for comparison of two cost-bounded reachability probabilities
on DTMCs

[
15
]
.

10
Conclusion and Future Work

The key insights for solving relational properties are as follows:

1.

RelReach
can be reduced to expected reward computations on the goal unfoldings with respect to the relevant target sets for each state-scheduler combination.

2.

RelBuechi
can be reduced to
RelReach
on (a slight variation of) the MEC quotient.

3.

MORelReach
can be reduced to multi-objective achievability queries with total expected reward objectives.

\Cref

tab:complexity_overview_full compares our complexity results for the three fragments over general schedulers; note that
\Cref
th:buechi_general_PTIME covers more general fragments of
RelBuechi
than those presented here.
We conjecture that we can extend our approach to multi-objective relational Büchi properties by combining the techniques for solving multi-objective relational reachability properties with those for solving relational Büchi properties.
We are currently working on extending our implementation to include the approach for solving
MORelReach
properties, more specifically for universally quantified disjunctive relational reachability properties using
\storm
’s MOA capabilities.

Future Work.

We are interested in how much we can extend this subclass of
HyperPCTL
while keeping the model-checking (decidable and even) tractable.
Since we can already handle Büchi and coBüchi conditions, an extension to Rabin conditions, i.e, disjunctions over pairs of a Büchi and a coBüchi condition, seems natural, thus extending our approach towards relational
\omega
-regular
properties.

Another step towards more expressive fragments would be
relational properties with
scheduler quantifier alternations
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.57 L 5.66 9.23 L 11.32 3.57 L 5.66 -2.09 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}T_{2})\Big)~.

Thus, properties of such forms can be solved by only minimizing and maximizing some expected reward, similarly to the case without quantifier alternation.
However, it is not immediately clear whether this generalizes to an arbitrary number of alternations.
In particular, one interesting question is whether the dependencies between the quantifiers are relevant: For a property of the form
\forall\sigma_{1}\exists\sigma_{2}.\ \varphi
, we may choose a different
\sigma_{2}
for each
\sigma_{1}
but, as illustrated above, we can solve such a property without considering the dependency between the two scheduler choices.

Further possible avenues for future work are to
allow nested probability operators,
relate properties that require
trace equivalence
,
consider relational
expected reward
or
long run average
properties
or models like
partially observable MDPs
with restricted scheduler classes.

B.1
Proof of
\Cref
thm:whyComb

See
10

Proof.

Quantifying over each state-scheduler combination individually is justified because of the following:
For every pair of distinct combinations
c=(s,\sigma),c^{\prime}=(s^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime})\in\mathscr{C}
it holds that either the scheduler variables are already distinct (i.e.,
\sigma\neq\sigma^{\prime}
), or else
\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}
and
s\neq s^{\prime}
.
In the latter case, since
memoryful schedulers may depend on the initial state
, we can instead quantify over two different schedulers.
More formally, the following is true in any MDP
\mathcal{M}
with states
s\neq s^{\prime}
(and target sets
T,T^{\prime}
):
For every
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}\in\Sigma^{\mathcal{M}}
there exists a (memoryful)
\hat{\sigma}\in\Sigma^{\mathcal{M}}
such that

{\text{Pr}}^{\sigma}_{s}(\operatorname{\hbox to8.7pt{\vbox to8.7pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.76527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.57 L 5.66 9.23 L 11.32 3.57 L 5.66 -2.09 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}T)={\text{Pr}}^{\hat{\sigma}}_{s}(\operatorname{\hbox to8.7pt{\vbox to8.7pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.76527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.57 L 5.66 9.23 L 11.32 3.57 L 5.66 -2.09 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}T)
and
{\text{Pr}}^{\sigma^{\prime}}_{s^{\prime}}(\operatorname{\hbox to8.7pt{\vbox to8.7pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.76527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.57 L 5.66 9.23 L 11.32 3.57 L 5.66 -2.09 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.57 L 5.66 9.23 L 11.32 3.57 L 5.66 -2.09 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}T^{\prime})
.
∎

B.2
Proof of
\Cref
thm:weightedReachProbsAsExpectedRew

See
15

Proof.

Let
c=(s,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\sigma})\in\mathscr{C}
and
\opt\in\{\min,\max\}
.
By construction, we have

\opt_{\sigma\in\Sigma^{\mathcal{M}}}\sum_{j\in\mathit{ind}(c)}q_{j}\cdot{\text{Pr}}_{s}^{\mathcal{M},\sigma}(\operatorname{\hbox to8.7pt{\vbox to8.7pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.76527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.57 L 5.66 9.23 L 11.32 3.57 L 5.66 -2.09 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}T_{j})=\opt_{\sigma\in\Sigma^{\mathcal{M}_{c}}}\sum_{j\in\mathit{ind}(c)}q_{j}\cdot{\text{Pr}}_{s_{c}}^{\mathcal{M}_{c},\sigma}(\operatorname{\hbox to8.7pt{\vbox to8.7pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.76527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.57 L 5.66 9.23 L 11.32 3.57 L 5.66 -2.09 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}T_{j})~.

Since
\textit{R}_{T_{j}}
collects reward 1 only on the first visit to
T_{j}
, it further follows that

\opt_{\sigma\in\Sigma^{\mathcal{M}_{c}}}\sum_{j\in\mathit{ind}(c)}q_{j}\cdot{\text{Pr}}_{s_{c}}^{\mathcal{M}_{c},\sigma}(\operatorname{\hbox to8.7pt{\vbox to8.7pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.76527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.57 L 5.66 9.23 L 11.32 3.57 L 5.66 -2.09 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}T_{j})=\opt_{\sigma\in\Sigma^{\mathcal{M}_{c}}}\sum_{j\in\mathit{ind}(c)}q_{j}\cdot\mathbb{E}^{\mathcal{M}_{c},\sigma}_{s_{c}}(\textit{R}_{T_{j}})~.

By linearity of expectations, it holds that

\displaystyle\opt_{\sigma\in\Sigma^{\mathcal{M}_{c}}}\sum_{j\in\mathit{ind}(c)}q_{j}\cdot\mathbb{E}^{\mathcal{M}_{c},\sigma}_{s_{c}}(\textit{R}_{T_{j}})

\displaystyle=\opt_{\sigma\in\Sigma^{\mathcal{M}_{c}}}\mathbb{E}^{\mathcal{M}_{c},\sigma}_{s_{c}}\left(\sum_{j\in\mathit{ind}(c)}q_{j}\cdot\textit{R}_{T_{j}}\right)

and finally

\displaystyle\opt_{\sigma\in\Sigma^{\mathcal{M}_{c}}}\mathbb{E}^{\mathcal{M}_{c},\sigma}_{s_{c}}\left(\sum_{j\in\mathit{ind}(c)}q_{j}\cdot\textit{R}_{T_{j}}\right)

\displaystyle=\opt_{\sigma\in\Sigma^{\mathcal{M}_{c}}}\mathbb{E}^{\mathcal{M}_{c},\sigma}_{s_{c}}(\textit{R}_{c})~,

since

\textit{R}_{c}=\sum_{T\in\mathcal{T}_{c}}(\sum_{j\in\{\mathit{ind}(c)\mid T=T_{j}\}}q_{j})\cdot\textit{R}_{T}=\sum_{j\in\mathit{ind}(c)}q_{j}\cdot\textit{R}_{T_{j}}
.
∎

B.3
Proof of
\Cref
thm:exRewPtimeMD

See
17

Proof.

Existence of MD optimal schedulers is a well-known result, see, e.g.
[
46
, Thm. 7.1.9]
.
To compute the expected rewards in polynomial time, we rely on
linear programming
(LP)
[
35
]
.
To express the
\max
-expected reward as the optimal solution of an LP we follow
[
51
, Ch. 4]
and first cast the expected total reward objective to an
expected reachability reward
objective
[
51
, Def. 2.27]
in a certain modified MDP
\mathcal{M}_{c}^{\prime}
, the so-called
MEC-quotient
of
\mathcal{M}_{c}
.
Intuitively,
\mathcal{M}_{c}^{\prime}
arises from
\mathcal{M}_{c}
by collapsing its MECs
E
into individual states
s_{E}
, and adding a fresh absorbing state
s_{\bot}
with an ingoing transition from every
s_{E}
to model the option of staying within
E
forever.
Since every scheduler eventually remains inside some MEC, the state
s_{\bot}
is
attracting
in
\mathcal{M}_{c}^{\prime}
, i.e.,
\forall\sigma\in\Sigma^{\mathcal{M}_{c}^{\prime}}.~{\text{Pr}}^{\sigma}(\operatorname{\hbox to8.7pt{\vbox to8.7pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.76527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.57 L 5.66 9.23 L 11.32 3.57 L 5.66 -2.09 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\{s_{\bot}\})=1
.
Next, observe that
\textit{R}_{c}
(
\Cref
def:rewForComb) assigns zero reward to all states inside the ECs of
\mathcal{M}_{c}
because reward is only collected upon reaching a target set
for the first time
.
We can thus naturally translate
\textit{R}_{c}
to a reward function
\textit{R}_{c}^{\prime}
in the quotient
\mathcal{M}_{c}^{\prime}
.

The optimal expected reward in
\mathcal{M}_{c}
is then equal to the optimal expected
reachability reward
w.r.t.
\{s_{\bot}\}
in
\mathcal{M}_{c}^{\prime}
, i.e., the expected reward collected until visiting
s_{\bot}
for the first time (see
[
51
, Sec. 4.1.5]
):

\opt_{\sigma\in\Sigma^{\mathcal{M}_{c}}}\mathbb{E}^{\mathcal{M}_{c},\sigma}_{s_{c}}(\textit{R}_{c})~=~\opt_{\sigma\in\Sigma^{\mathcal{M}_{c}^{\prime}}}\mathbb{E}^{\mathcal{M}_{c}^{\prime},\sigma}_{s_{c}}(\textit{R}_{c}^{\prime}\operatorname{\hbox to8.7pt{\vbox to8.7pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.76527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.57 L 5.66 9.23 L 11.32 3.57 L 5.66 -2.09 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\{s_{\bot}\})

Since
\{s_{\bot}\}
is attracting, the
Bellman-equations
associated with the above reachability reward objective in
\mathcal{M}_{c}^{\prime}
have a
unique solution

[
51
, Thm. 4.8]
and can thus be readily expressed as an LP of size linear in the size of
\mathcal{M}_{c}^{\prime}

[
51
, Fig. 4.10]
.

The above approach applies to
\min
-expected rewards as well by noticing that in any MDP
\mathcal{M}
with a state
s
and a reward function
R
such that the optimal expected rewards are well-defined,
\min_{\sigma\in\Sigma^{\mathcal{M}}}\mathbb{E}_{s}^{\sigma}(\textit{R})=-\max_{\sigma\in\Sigma^{\mathcal{M}}}\mathbb{E}_{s}^{\sigma}(-\textit{R})
.
∎

B.4
Proof of
\Cref
thm:cruxApproxEqual

See
20

Proof of
\Cref

We show

\Rightarrow
”
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.57 L 5.66 9.23 L 11.32 3.57 L 5.66 -2.09 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

both hold in
\mathcal{M}
.
Let
\sigma_{1}^{\geq},\ldots,\sigma_{n}^{\geq}
and
\sigma_{1}^{\leq},\ldots,\sigma_{n}^{\leq}
be witnessing schedulers for the two properties.
Further, let
\overline{v}\geq q-\epsilon
and
\underline{v}\leq q+\epsilon
be the weighted sums of probabilities induced by these schedulers.
We distinguish two cases:

First, if we have
\overline{v}\leq q+\epsilon

or

\underline{v}\geq q-\epsilon
, then
\sigma_{1}^{\geq},\ldots,\sigma_{n}^{\geq}
or
\sigma_{1}^{\leq},\ldots,\sigma_{n}^{\leq}
are already witnessing schedulers for the property and we are done.

Otherwise, we have
\overline{v}>q+\epsilon

and

\underline{v}<q-\epsilon
.
This means that
q\in(\underline{v},\overline{v})
and thus there exists
\lambda\in(0,1)
such that
q=\lambda\underline{v}+(1-\lambda)\overline{v}
.
The crux is now to consider the schedulers
\sigma_{i}^{\lambda}=[\sigma^{\leq}_{i}\oplus_{\lambda}\sigma^{\geq}_{i}]
,
i=1,\ldots,n
(for details, see
[
51
, p. 71]
).
These schedulers satisfy

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{m}q_{i}\cdot{\text{Pr}}^{\sigma_{k_{i}}^{\lambda}}_{s_{i}}(\operatorname{\hbox to8.7pt{\vbox to8.7pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.76527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.57 L 5.66 9.23 L 11.32 3.57 L 5.66 -2.09 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}T_{i})~=~

\displaystyle\lambda\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{m}q_{i}\cdot{\text{Pr}}^{\sigma_{k_{i}}^{\leq}}_{s_{i}}(\operatorname{\hbox to8.7pt{\vbox to8.7pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.76527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

\displaystyle~=~

) with

).

We show

\Leftarrow
”
by contraposition.
Assume that
q<v^{\min}-\epsilon
(the argument for the other case
q>v^{\min}+\epsilon
is analogous).
It follows that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}

\lxSVG@closescope }

, i.e., all schedulers give a value at least

apart from

Appendix C
Proofs for
\Cref
sec:reach_complexity

The proofs in this section are taken verbatim from
[
27
]
.

C.1
Proof of
\Cref
th:general_PTIME

See
24

Proof.

(a)
:
m
is the only parameter occurring exponentially in the runtime complexity of
\Cref
alg:linear_general with exact reward computation.

(b)
: If all target sets are absorbing, then for each
c\in\mathscr{C}
, the number of reachable states in
\mathcal{M}_{c}
is
|(S\times\emptyset)\cup\bigcup_{i}T_{i}\times\{T_{i}\}|=|S|+\sum_{i=1}^{m}|T_{i}|\leq(m+1)\cdot|S|
.
Hence, the size of the unfolding is linear in the size of the original MDP and
m
.

(c)
: If each probability operator is evaluated under a different scheduler, then
|\mathscr{C}|=n=m
and for each
c\in\mathscr{C}
we have
|\mathit{ind}(c)|=1
. Hence, for each
c\in\mathscr{C}
, we have
|S_{c}|=|S\times 2^{\mathit{ind}(c)}|=|S|\cdot 2
and thus the size of the unfolding with respect to
\mathit{ind}(c)
is linear in the size of the original MDP.
∎

C.2
Proof of
\Cref
th:MD_NP-complete

See
25

We show
NP
-hardness by giving a polynomial transformation from the Hamiltonian path problem,
which is known to be strongly
NP
-hard
[
26
]
, inspired by
[
23
]
.
For exact equality, we establish strong
NP
-hardness by showing that our transformation is pseudo-polynomial.

Definition 87

(Hamiltonian Path Problem)
.

Given a directed graph
G=(V,E)

(where
V
is a set of vertices and
E\subseteq V\times V
a set of edges)
and some initial vertex
v\in V
,
decide whether there exists a path from
v
in
G
that visits each vertex exactly once.

{subfigure}

[b]0.3

v_{\textit{init}}
v_{1}
v_{2}
v_{3}

Figure 24:
Graph
G=(V,E)

{subfigure}

[t]0.7

v_{\textit{init}}
v_{1}
v_{2}
v_{3}
s_{\bot}
s_{a}
s_{1}
⋮
s_{|V|}
s_{\scriptscriptstyle|V|-1}
s_{b}
s_{0}

Figure 25:
MDP constructed from
G
and initial vertex
v_{0}
. All distributions are uniform.

Figure 26:
Illustration of the MDP construction for the reduction from the Hamiltonian path problem.

Proof of
\Cref
th:MD_NP-complete.

Membership: Given some
RelReach
property, MDP
\mathcal{M}
and a memoryless deterministic scheduler
\sigma\in\Sigma^{\mathcal{M}}
, we can verify whether
\sigma
is a witness for the property
by computing the (exact) reachability probabilities in the induced DTMC, see e.g.,
[
8
, Ch. 10]
.
This is possible in time polynomial in the size of the state space
[
11
]
.

NP
-hardness:
We first give the reduction for
(a)
with
s_{1}=s_{2}

and explain how to adjust the construction for the other cases afterwards.
For a given instance of the Hamiltonian path problem
G=(V,E)
and
v_{\textit{init}}\in V
,
we construct the MDP
\mathcal{M}=(S,\allowbreak\textit{Act},\allowbreak\mathbf{P})
with

•

S=V\cup\{s_{0},s_{\bot},s_{a},s_{b}\}\cup\{s_{i}\mid i=1,\ldots,|V|-1\}

•

\textit{Act}=E\cup\{\tau\}

•

\mathbf{P}(s_{0},\tau,v_{\textit{init}})=\frac{1}{2}
and
\mathbf{P}(s_{0},\tau,s_{1})=\frac{1}{2}

\mathbf{P}(s_{i},\tau,s_{i+1})=\frac{1}{2}
and
\mathbf{P}(s_{i},\tau,s_{\bot})=\frac{1}{2}
for
i=1,\ldots,|V|-2

\mathbf{P}(s_{|V|-1},\tau,s_{b})=\frac{1}{2}
and
\mathbf{P}(s_{|V|-1},\tau,s_{\bot})=\frac{1}{2}

\mathbf{P}(v,(v,v^{\prime}),v^{\prime})=\frac{1}{2}
and
\mathbf{P}(v,(v,v^{\prime}),s_{\bot})=\frac{1}{2}
for all
v,v^{\prime}\in V,(v,v^{\prime})\in E

\mathbf{P}(v,\tau,s_{a})=1
for all
v\in V

\mathbf{P}(s_{\bot},\tau,s_{\bot})=1
,
\mathbf{P}(s_{a},\tau,s_{a})=1
and
\mathbf{P}(s_{b},\tau,s_{b})=1

\mathbf{P}(s,\alpha,s^{\prime})=0
otherwise

\Cref

fig:ill_ham-path_reduction illustrates the construction:

\Cref
fig:ill_ham-path_reduction_mdp shows the MDP constructed from the graph
G
with initial vertex
v_{0}
depicted in
\Cref
fig:ill_ham-path_reduction_graph.

We observe that for all schedulers
\sigma\in\Sigma^{\mathcal{M}}_{\textit{MD}}
, it holds that
{\text{Pr}}^{\sigma}_{s_{0}}(\operatorname{\hbox to8.7pt{\vbox to8.7pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.76527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.57 L 5.66 9.23 L 11.32 3.57 L 5.66 -2.09 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\{s_{b}\})=\frac{1}{2^{|V|}}
.
Further, for all schedulers
\sigma\in\Sigma^{\mathcal{M}}_{\textit{MD}}
, we have

\displaystyle{{\text{Pr}}}^{\sigma}_{s_{0}}(\operatorname{\hbox to8.7pt{\vbox to8.7pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.76527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\{s_{b}\})|=|{\text{Pr}}^{\sigma}_{s_{0}}(\operatorname{\hbox to8.7pt{\vbox to8.7pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.76527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.57 L 5.66 9.23 L 11.32 3.57 L 5.66 -2.09 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\{s_{b}\})|=\frac{1}{2^{|V|}}
, which is a contradiction.
So
s_{a}
is reachable from
s_{0}
, and hence also from
v_{\textit{init}}
and the finite path from
v_{\textit{init}}
to
s_{a}
cannot contain a loop since since
\sigma
is memoryless deterministic.
Let
n
be the number of
V
-states on the path from
v_{\textit{init}}
to
s_{a}
under
\sigma
(not counting
v_{\textit{init}}
itself).
Then
{\text{Pr}}^{\sigma}_{s_{0}}(\operatorname{\hbox to8.7pt{\vbox to8.7pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.76527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.57 L 5.66 9.23 L 11.32 3.57 L 5.66 -2.09 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }

.
Since

, this implies that
{\text{Pr}}^{\sigma}_{s_{0}}(\operatorname{\hbox to8.7pt{\vbox to8.7pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.76527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.57 L 5.66 9.23 L 11.32 3.57 L 5.66 -2.09 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\{s_{a}\})\geq\frac{1}{2^{|V|}}
.
Further,

\displaystyle|{\text{Pr}}^{\sigma}_{s_{0}}(\operatorname{\hbox to8.7pt{\vbox to8.7pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.76527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

, this implies

{\text{Pr}}^{\sigma}_{s_{0}}(\operatorname{\hbox to8.7pt{\vbox to8.7pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.76527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

From
\frac{1}{2^{|V|-1}}>{\text{Pr}}^{\sigma}_{s_{0}}(\operatorname{\hbox to8.7pt{\vbox to8.7pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.76527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.57 L 5.66 9.23 L 11.32 3.57 L 5.66 -2.09 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\{s_{a}\})=\frac{1}{2^{n+1}}\geq\frac{1}{2^{|V|}}
we can conclude that
n=|V|-1
.
Hence, the path from
v_{\textit{init}}
to
s_{a}
visits
|V|-1
states corresponding to vertices in
G
, which means it must visit each vertex of
G
exactly once and thus corresponds to a Hamiltonian path from
v_{\textit{init}}
in
G
.

\proofsubparagraph

Claim:
The above construction defines a pseudo-polynomial time transformation to
(a)
, and a polynomial-time transformation to
(a)
with approximate equality with
\epsilon>0
.

The constructed MDP has
2\cdot|V|+3
states and
|E|+1
actions. Each state has at most two successors.
All transition probabilities in the MDP are either
0
,
0.5
, or
1
. Hence, the magnitude of the largest number occurring in the constructed MDP is a constant and thus polynomial in the size of the original graph.

For approximate equality, we must choose some
0<\epsilon<\frac{1}{2^{|V|+1}}
for the constructed property.
The magnitude of the largest number occurring in the constructed
RelReach
instance thus depends exponentially on the size of the original Hamiltonian path problem instance.
Hence, the transformation is not pseudo-polynomial.
The transformation is, however, still polynomial since clearly
\epsilon<1
and hence all numbers are polynomially bounded.

For exact equality, however,
\epsilon=0
and hence the magnitude of the largest number occurring in the constructed
RelReach
instance is polynomial in the size of the Hamiltonian path problem instance.
Hence, the transformation is pseudo-polynomial.

We construct the property

“
\exists\sigma\in\Sigma^{\mathcal{M}}_{\textit{MD}}.\ {\text{Pr}}^{\sigma}_{v_{0}}(\operatorname{\hbox to8.7pt{\vbox to8.7pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.76527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}

”
.
Observe that for all schedulers
\sigma\in\Sigma^{\mathcal{M}}_{\textit{MD}}
, it holds that
{\text{Pr}}^{\sigma}_{s_{1}}(\operatorname{\hbox to8.7pt{\vbox to8.7pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.76527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

.
The proof works analogously.

Proof.

We first show that randomization is necessary:
Consider the MDP in
\Cref
fig:randomization-necessary_app.
Over general schedulers, the property

“
\exists\sigma.\ {\text{Pr}}^{\sigma}_{s}(\operatorname{\hbox to8.7pt{\vbox to8.7pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.76527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.57 L 5.66 9.23 L 11.32 3.57 L 5.66 -2.09 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\{t\})\approx_{\epsilon}0.5
”
holds for any
\epsilon
, but over (possibly memoryful) deterministic schedulers it holds only for
\epsilon\geq 0.5
.

We further see that memory is also necessary, i.e., there are instances where memoryless randomized schedulers do not suffice:
Consider the MDP in
\Cref
fig:memory-necessary_app.
Over general schedulers, the property

“
\exists\sigma_{1}.\ {\text{Pr}}^{\sigma}_{s}(\operatorname{\hbox to8.7pt{\vbox to8.7pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.76527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}

\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\{t_{1}\})={\text{Pr}}^{\sigma}_{s}(\operatorname{\hbox to8.7pt{\vbox to8.7pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.76527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.57 L 5.66 9.23 L 11.32 3.57 L 5.66 -2.09 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\{t_{2}\})
”
holds,
but over memoryless schedulers it does not.
Consider a memoryless randomized scheduler choosing
\alpha
with probability
p
. If
p=1
then the property does not hold. If
p<1
then
{\text{Pr}}^{\sigma}_{s}(\operatorname{\hbox to8.7pt{\vbox to8.7pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.76527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.57 L 5.66 9.23 L 11.32 3.57 L 5.66 -2.09 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\{t_{1}\})=p\neq{\text{Pr}}^{\sigma}_{s}(\operatorname{\hbox to8.7pt{\vbox to8.7pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.76527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.57 L 5.66 9.23 L 11.32 3.57 L 5.66 -2.09 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\{t_{2}\})=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}p^{i}(1-p)=\frac{1-p}{1-p}=1
.

C.4
Proof of
\Cref
th:MD_PTIME

See
28

Proof.

For each case, we show that
\Cref
alg:linear_general returns an MD scheduler and runs in polynomial time.

\proofsubparagraph

(a)
:
If
n=m
and
\bowtie\in\{\geq,>,\not\approx_{\epsilon}\mid\epsilon\geq 0\}
,
then
|\mathit{ind}(c)|=1
for each
c\in\mathscr{C}
and each scheduler
\sigma_{i}\in\{\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{n}\}
corresponds to an MD scheduler for
\mathcal{M}_{c}
for the unique
c\in\mathscr{C}
with
c=(-,\sigma_{i})
.
Further, any MD scheduler on
\mathcal{M}_{c}
can be transformed back to an MD scheduler for
\mathcal{M}
since there is only a single target set.

We have already argued in
\Cref
th:general_PTIME that
\Cref
alg:linear_general runs in polynomial time in this case.

\proofsubparagraph

(b)
:
We show the claim for the simplest case, namely
n=1
and
s_{1}=\ldots=s_{m}
and all target sets are absorbing and
\bowtie\in\{\geq,>,\not\approx_{\epsilon}\mid\epsilon\geq 0\}
.
Then
|\mathscr{C}|=1
, so there is a unique
c\in\mathscr{C}
.
We can transform any MD scheduler for
\mathcal{M}_{c}
back to an MD scheduler on
\mathcal{M}
since the target sets are absorbing and there is a unique initial state
s_{1}=\ldots=s_{m}
.

Further, since target states are absorbing, the size of the goal unfolding
\mathcal{M}_{c}
is linear in
\mathcal{M}
and hence
\Cref
alg:linear_general runs in polynomial time.

The reasoning for the general case follows analogously.

\proofsubparagraph

(c)
:
We show the claim for the simplest case, namely
n=1
and
T_{1}=\ldots=T_{m}=:T
and for all
i=1,\ldots,m
we have
q_{i}\geq 0
and
\bowtie\in\{\geq,>,\not\approx_{\epsilon}\mid\epsilon\geq 0\}
.
We know that there exists some MD scheduler
\sigma^{\max}\in\Sigma^{\mathcal{M}}_{\textit{MD}}
maximizing the reachability probability of
T
for all
s\in S
.
Since all coefficients
q_{1},\ldots,q_{m}
are non-negative, it follows that

\displaystyle\exists\sigma\in\Sigma^{\mathcal{M}}.\ \sum_{i=1}^{m}q_{i}\cdot{\text{Pr}}^{\sigma}_{s_{i}}(\operatorname{\hbox to8.7pt{\vbox to8.7pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.76527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.57 L 5.66 9.23 L 11.32 3.57 L 5.66 -2.09 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}T)\geq q_{m+1}\quad\mathbin{\textit{ iff }}\quad

and analogously for
>
and
\not\approx_{\epsilon}
.
Hence,
\Cref
alg:linear_general returns true iff the property is satisfiable over MD schedulers.

Further, since
T_{1}=\ldots=T_{m}
, the size of the goal unfolding is linear in the size of the original MDP and hence
\Cref
alg:linear_general runs in polynomial time.

The reasoning for the general case follows analogously.
∎

Appendix D
Proofs for
\Cref
sec:buechi_algo

D.1
Proof of
\Cref
th:relBuechi-to-relReach

Let us fix some notation needed for both directions of
\Cref
th:relBuechi-to-relReach.
For
\pi\in\textit{Paths}^{\mathcal{M}}
, we let
\textit{Limit}(\pi)
be the tuple
(S^{\prime},A)
where
S^{\prime}
is the set of all states that are visited infinitely often in
\pi
and
A
is the set of all actions that are taken infinitely often in
\pi
(cf., e.g.,
[
8
]
).

Let
c\in\mathscr{C}
and
q_{c}\in Q
.
For
\mathcal{T}\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{c}
, we use
T\not\in\mathcal{T}
as shorthand for
T\in\mathcal{T}_{c}\setminus\mathcal{T}
.
Recall that for
j,j^{\prime}\in\mathit{ind}(c)
we have
s_{j}=s_{j^{\prime}}
; we use
s_{0}
to denote this unique state.

For
\sigma\in\Sigma^{\mathcal{M}}
and
\pi\in\textit{Paths}_{\textit{fin}}^{\mathcal{M}}
we define the
residual scheduler

\sigma(\pi)
as the scheduler behaving like
\sigma
after having seen
\pi\in\textit{Paths}_{\textit{fin}}^{\mathcal{M}}
.

For
s,t\in S
we use
\pi\colon s\to t
to denote
\pi\in\textit{Paths}_{\textit{fin}}^{\mathcal{M}}(s)
with
\textit{last}(\pi)=t
.

Definition 88

(Residual scheduler)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be an MDP and
\sigma\in\Sigma^{\mathcal{M}}
.
Let
\pi\in\textit{Paths}_{\textit{fin}}^{\mathcal{M}}
.
Then we define the
residual scheduler of
\sigma
after
\pi
as the (partial) scheduler
\sigma(\pi)\in\Sigma^{\mathcal{M}}
with

\sigma(\pi)(s_{1}\alpha_{1}\ldots\alpha_{r-1}s_{r})=\sigma(\pi\alpha_{1}\ldots\alpha_{r-1}s_{r})

for
s_{1}\alpha_{1}\ldots\alpha_{r-1}s_{r}\in\textit{Paths}_{\textit{fin}}^{\mathcal{M}}
with
s_{1}=\textit{last}(\pi)
, and we let
\sigma(\pi)
undefined for
s_{1}\neq\textit{last}(\pi)
.

We define a correspondence between paths of
\mathcal{M}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}
as in
[
9
]
.
For
\pi\in\textit{Paths}^{\mathcal{M}}
, let
f(\pi)
be the unique path corresponding to
\pi
in
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}
.
For
\pi\in\textit{Paths}_{\textit{fin}}^{\mathcal{M}}
, let
f(\pi)
be the unique path corresponding to
\pi
in
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}
that does not contain a state from
S_{\bot}
.
Conversely, for
\widehat{\pi}\in\textit{Paths}^{\widehat{\mathcal{M}}_{T_{i}}}
, let
\widehat{f}(\widehat{\pi})

be the set of paths in
\mathcal{M}
that correspond to
\widehat{\pi}
, such that the last action does not belong to the same MEC as the last state (i.e., if the last state of the path is in some MEC, then we must have only just entered this MEC).

For paths ending in a state
\bot^{\mathcal{T}}\in S_{\bot}
for some
\mathcal{T}\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{c}
for some
c\in\mathscr{C}
, we choose the unique action
\epsilon^{\mathcal{T}}
(observe that
\widehat{\textit{Act}}(\bot^{\mathcal{T}})=\{\epsilon^{\mathcal{T}}\}
).

We can convince ourselves that
\widehat{\sigma}
is well-defined, i.e.,
\sum_{\alpha\in\widehat{\textit{Act}}}\widehat{\sigma}(\widehat{\pi},\alpha)=1
for all
\widehat{\pi}\in\textit{Paths}_{\textit{fin}}^{\widehat{\mathcal{M}}}(f(s_{0}))
.

By induction on the length of
\widehat{\pi}
, we can show that for all
\widehat{\pi}\in\textit{Paths}_{\textit{fin}}^{\widehat{\mathcal{M}}}(f(s_{0}))
with
\textit{last}(\widehat{\pi})\not\in S_{\bot}
we have

{\text{Pr}}^{\widehat{\mathcal{M}},\widehat{\sigma}}_{f(s_{0})}(\widehat{\pi})=\sum_{\pi\in\widehat{f}(\widehat{\pi})}{\text{Pr}}^{\mathcal{M},\sigma}_{s_{0}}(\pi)
.

The last equality holds due to the correspondence between the probability of a finite path

Proof of
\Cref
th:relBuechi-to-relReach_Leftarrow

We first show the claim for the case that the given scheduler for the quotient
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}
is memoryless in
\Cref
th:relBuechi-to-relReach_Leftarrow_MR and then prove that this suffices to show the desired claim.

For the scheduler witness transfer from
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}
to
\mathcal{M}
, it is convenient to use a scheduler representation with explicit memory modes
instead of the path-based version introduced in
\Cref
sec:prelim, we schematically illustrate the new definition in
\Cref
fig:sched_mem_illustration.

Definition 89

(Explicit-memory scheduler representation)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=(S,\allowbreak\textit{Act},\allowbreak\mathbf{P})
be an MDP.
An
scheduler
\sigma
for
\mathcal{M}
in explicit-memory representation
is a tuple
(Q,\textit{init},\textit{mode},\textit{act})
with

•

Q
a countable set of memory modes,

•

\textit{init}\in\textit{Distr}(Q)
a stochastic initial mode selection function,

•

\textit{mode}\colon Q\times\textit{Act}\times S\to\textit{Distr}(Q)
a stochastic memory update function, where
\textit{mode}(q,\alpha,s^{\prime})(q^{\prime})
gives the probability of updating the memory to
q^{\prime}\in Q
if the current mode is
q\in Q
, we chose action
\alpha
in the current state and moved to
s^{\prime}
,
and

•

\textit{act}\colon Q\times S\to\textit{Distr}(\textit{Act})
a stochastic action selection function, where
\textit{act}(q,s)(\alpha)
is the probability of choosing
\alpha
in state
s
and mode
q
.

s
\alpha
s^{\prime}
q
q^{\prime}
init
act
mode
\mathbf{P}

Figure 30:
Illustration of interaction of
init
,
mode
,
act
, and
\mathbf{P}
for explicit-memory scheduler representation (
\Cref
def:scheduler_explicit)

There is a direct correspondence between the explicit-memory and path-based scheduler representations for some given initial state
s_{0}
.

Lemma 90
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=(S,\allowbreak\textit{Act},\allowbreak\mathbf{P})
be an MDP and
s_{0}\in S
.
For every
\sigma=(Q,\textit{init},\textit{mode},\textit{act})
there exists
\sigma^{\prime}\colon\textit{Paths}_{\textit{fin}}^{\mathcal{M}}\to\textit{Distr}(\textit{Act})
such that

{\text{Pr}}^{\mathcal{M},\sigma}_{s_{0}}(\pi)={\text{Pr}}^{\mathcal{M},\sigma^{\prime}}_{s_{0}}(\pi)

for any finite path
\pi\in\textit{Paths}_{\textit{fin}}^{\mathcal{M}}(s_{0})
,
and vice versa.

Proof.

‘
\Rightarrow
’:
Given
\sigma=(Q,\textit{init},\textit{mode},\textit{act})
, we recursively define

•

for
s\in S
,
q\in Q
,
\alpha\in\textit{Act}(s)
,
\pi\beta s^{\prime}\in\textit{Paths}_{\textit{fin}}^{\mathcal{M}}
:

\textit{mode}(s)(q)=\textit{init}(q)\qquad\textit{mode}(\pi\beta s^{\prime})(q)=\sum_{q^{\prime}\in Q}\textit{mode}(q^{\prime},s^{\prime},\beta)(q)\cdot\textit{mode}(\pi)(q^{\prime})

•

for
\pi\in\textit{Paths}_{\textit{fin}}^{\mathcal{M}}
,
\alpha\in\textit{Act}(s)
:

\textit{act}(\pi)(\alpha)=\sum_{q\in Q}\textit{act}(q,\textit{last}(\pi))(\alpha)\cdot\textit{mode}(\pi)(q)

We define
\sigma^{\prime}\colon\textit{Paths}_{\textit{fin}}^{\mathcal{M}}\to\textit{Distr}(\textit{Act})
with
\sigma^{\prime}(\pi)=\textit{act}(\pi)
for
\pi\in\textit{Paths}_{\textit{fin}}^{\mathcal{M}}
.

‘
\Leftarrow
’:
Given a scheduler
\sigma^{\prime}\colon\textit{Paths}_{\textit{fin}}^{\mathcal{M}}\to\textit{Distr}(\textit{Act})
we construct
\sigma=(Q,\textit{init},\textit{mode},\textit{act})
with

•

Q=\textit{Paths}_{\textit{fin}}^{\mathcal{M}}(s_{0})

•

\textit{init}(s_{0})=1
and
\textit{init}(q)=0
for
q\in Q\setminus\{s_{0}\}

•

\textit{mode}(s_{0}\alpha_{0}\ldots s_{n},\alpha,s^{\prime})(q)=[q=s_{0}\alpha_{0}\ldots s_{n}]

for
s_{0}\alpha_{0}\ldots s_{n}\in\textit{Paths}_{\textit{fin}}^{\mathcal{M}}(s_{0})
,
\alpha\in\textit{Act}(s_{n})
,
s^{\prime}\in S
and
q\in Q

•

\textit{act}(s_{0}\alpha_{0}\ldots s_{n},s_{n})=\sigma^{\prime}(s_{0}\alpha_{0}\ldots s_{n})
for
s_{0}\alpha_{0}\ldots s_{n}\in\textit{Paths}_{\textit{fin}}^{\mathcal{M}}(s_{0})

22
22

22

Technically, we would also need to define
\textit{act}(s_{0}\alpha_{0}\ldots s_{n},q)
for
q\neq s_{n}
; we can choose any arbitrary distribution.

∎

Note that the transfer from explicit-memory to path-based representation does not depend on a given initial state, but the other direction does.

In the following, for an explicit-memory scheduler
\sigma
we may sometimes use
\sigma(\pi)
to denote
\sigma^{\prime}(\pi)
for the induced path-based scheduler
\sigma^{\prime}
from the construction above under abuse of notation.

See
45

Proof.

Let
\widehat{\sigma}\in\Sigma^{\widehat{\mathcal{M}}}_{\textit{MR}}
.
We construct a scheduler
\sigma\in\Sigma^{\mathcal{M}}
analogously to
[
7
, pp. 10–11]
.

Recall that
s_{j}=s_{j^{\prime}}
for
j,j^{\prime}\in\mathit{ind}(c)
.

Intuitively, when entering an MEC
C
we flip a coin and either switch to some scheduler staying in
C
forever, or to some scheduler leaving
C
via the action chosen by
\widehat{\sigma}
.
More precisely, for
\mathcal{T}\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{c}
for some
c\in\mathscr{C}
, with probability
\widehat{\sigma}(s_{c})(\epsilon^{\mathcal{T}})
we switch to a memoryless scheduler going to a sub-EC
C^{\prime}
of
C
with
C^{\prime}\in\textit{EC}_{\mathcal{T}}
.
With the remaining probability
1-\sum_{\mathcal{T}\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{c},c\in\mathscr{C}}\widehat{\sigma}(s_{c})(\epsilon^{\mathcal{T}})
, we switch to a memoryless scheduler that leaves
C
almost-surely, while mimicking the probabilities with which
\widehat{\sigma}
chooses each outgoing action of
C
.
The construction of the latter is technically involved and we refer to
[
7
]
for details on the formal construction.

We define
\sigma
as a tuple
(Q,\textit{init},\textit{mode},\textit{act})
where

•

Q=\{q_{0}\}\cup\{q_{\mathcal{T}}\mid\mathcal{T}\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{c},c\in\mathscr{C}\}
.

In the following, we use
\mu_{C}\in\textit{Distr}(Q)
to denote the distribution defined by
\mu_{C}(q_{0})=1-\sum_{\mathcal{T}\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{c},c\in\mathscr{C}}\widehat{\sigma}(s_{C})(\epsilon^{\mathcal{T}})
and
\mu_{C}(q_{\mathcal{T}})=\widehat{\sigma}(s_{C})(\epsilon^{\mathcal{T}})
for
\mathcal{T}\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{c},c\in\mathscr{C}
.

•

\textit{init}\colon Q\to[0,1]
defined as follows.
If there exists some
C_{0}\in\textit{MEC}(\mathcal{M})
containing
s_{0}
then

\textit{init}=\mu_{C_{0}}
.
Otherwise
\textit{init}(q)=[q=q_{0}]
for
q\in Q
.

•

\textit{mode}\colon Q\times\textit{Act}\times S\to\textit{Distr}(Q)
with

\textit{mode}(q,\alpha,s)=\begin{cases}\mu_{C}&\text{if }\exists C=(S^{\prime},A)\in\textit{MEC}(\mathcal{M}).\ s\in S^{\prime}\wedge\alpha\not\in A\\
(q\mapsto 1)&\text{otherwise}~.\end{cases}

Intuitively, we flip a coin when entering an MEC to go to
q_{0}
or some
q_{I}
.
Inside MECs and on states outside MECs, we do not update the memory.

•

\textit{act}\colon S\times Q\to\textit{Distr}(\textit{Act})
defined as follows:

–

For
s\in S\setminus S_{\textit{MEC}}
and
q\in Q
:

\textit{act}(s,q)=\widehat{\sigma}(s)

–

For
s\in S
where there exists some
C=(S^{\prime},A)\in\textit{MEC}(\mathcal{M})
with
s\in S^{\prime}
:

\displaystyle\textit{act}(s,q_{0})

\displaystyle=\sigma_{\textsf{leave},C}(s)

\displaystyle\textit{act}(s,q_{\mathcal{T}})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\sigma_{C,\mathcal{T}}(s)&\text{if }C\in\textit{MEC}_{\mathcal{T}}\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\text{ for }\mathcal{T}\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{c},c\in\mathscr{C}

where for
C=(S^{\prime},A)\in\textit{MEC}(\mathcal{M})
:

–

\sigma_{\textsf{leave},C}
leaves
C
almost surely while mimicking the probabilities with which
\widehat{\sigma}
chooses each outgoing action of
C
, i.e.,
\alpha\in\widehat{\textit{Act}}(s_{c})
.
Formally, let
p_{C}=1-\sum_{I\subseteq[m]}\widehat{\sigma}(s_{c})(\epsilon^{I})
for
C\in\textit{MEC}(\mathcal{M})
, then the scheduler satisfies the following constraint for
\widehat{\pi}=\widehat{\pi^{\prime}}\beta s_{C}\in\textit{Paths}_{\textit{fin}}^{\widehat{\mathcal{M}}}(f(s_{0}))
,
s\in S^{\prime}
and
\alpha\in\textit{Act}(s)\setminus A
:

\left[\sum_{\pi\in\widehat{f}(\widehat{\pi})}\hskip-4.30554pt{\text{Pr}}_{s_{0}}^{\sigma}(\pi)\hskip-12.91663pt\sum_{\pi^{\prime}\colon\textit{last}(\pi)\xrightarrow{C}s}\hskip-17.22217pt{\text{Pr}}_{\textit{last}(\pi)}^{\sigma(\pi)}(\pi^{\prime})\right]\sigma_{\textsf{leave},C}(s)(\alpha)p_{C}=\left[\sum_{\pi\in\widehat{f}(\widehat{\pi})}\hskip-4.30554pt{\text{Pr}}_{s_{0}}^{\sigma}(\pi)\right]\widehat{\sigma}(s_{C})(\alpha)

(D.1)

where we use
\pi^{\prime}\colon\textit{last}(\pi)\xrightarrow{C}s
as shorthand for
\pi\in\{s_{0}\alpha_{0}\ldots s_{n}\in\textit{Paths}_{\textit{fin}}^{\mathcal{M}}(\textit{last}(\pi))\mid s_{n}=s\wedge\forall i<n.\ s_{i}\in S^{\prime}\wedge\alpha_{i}\in A\}
.
For a formal construction of
\sigma_{\textsf{leave},C}
we refer to
[
7
, p. 10–11]
with
p_{C}=1-\sum_{I\subseteq[m]}\widehat{\sigma}(s_{c})(\epsilon^{I})
.

–

\sigma_{C,\mathcal{T}}
ensures that we stay in
C
and see exactly the target sets
T\in\mathcal{T}
infinitely often.
Such a scheduler must exist with the following reasoning:
By construction, for each
\mathcal{T}\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{c}
with
C\in\textit{MEC}_{\mathcal{T}}
, there must exist some sub-EC
C^{\prime}
of
C
that (1) contains at least state from each
T\in\mathcal{T}
and (2) contains no state from any
T\in\mathcal{T}_{c}\setminus\mathcal{T}
.
Hence, there must exist some memoryless deterministic scheduler
\sigma_{C,\mathcal{T}}
on
\mathcal{M}
restricted to
C
that almost-surely goes to
C^{\prime}
, and stays there forever.

Observe that
q_{0}
and
q_{\emptyset}
have different purposes: In
q_{0}
, we aim to leave
C
while in
q_{\emptyset}
we aim to stay in
C
without seeing any target set infinitely often.

We can convince ourselves that
\sigma
is well-defined.

We can show that for all
\widehat{\pi}_{T_{i}}\in\textit{Paths}_{\textit{fin}}^{\widehat{\mathcal{M}}_{T_{i}}}(f(s_{0}))
with
\textit{last}(\widehat{\pi}_{T_{i}})\not\in S_{\bot}
it holds that

{\text{Pr}}^{\widehat{\mathcal{M}}_{T_{i}},\widehat{\sigma}_{T_{i}}}_{f(s_{0})}(\widehat{\pi}_{T_{i}})=\sum_{\pi\in\widehat{f}_{T_{i}}(\widehat{\pi}_{T_{i}})}{\text{Pr}}^{\mathcal{M},\sigma}_{s_{0}}(\pi)
, by induction on the length of
\widehat{\pi}_{T_{i}}
, using (
D.1
).

\sigma
satisfies the desired claim.

’ holds almost-surely. No other

after

We now observe that

holds for all

but

and hence
